TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

India is a logical Russia-Ukraine mediator . It has a deep, long-standing partnership with Moscow and friendly relations with Kyiv. It also has solid ties with other key third-party actors, such as the European Union and the Arab states; its relations with Washington—despite tensions during in President Donald Trump’s second term—remain workable. Further, India has a strong interest in the war coming to an end. A longer conflict could wear down Russia’s capacity to export oil and weapons to India. It could also increase Moscow’s dependence on Beijing, a strategic competitor of New Delhi. The conflict also creates headaches for Indian foreign policy, complicating efforts to balance ties with Russia and the West. The highlights this week: Tanzania’s opposition leader is sentenced to prison, the African National Congress suffers historic losses in South Africa’s local elections, and the Ethiopia-Sudan rift deepens. One year after Tanzania’s disputed October 2025 presidential election, the country remains in a political crisis. Incumbent President Samia Suluhu Hassan ran largely uncontested, winning 98 percent of the vote after the country’s electoral body disqualified her main opponents. Her second term began with a deadly crackdown on protesters by state security forces that saw more than 1,000 people killed, according to rights groups. Since before the elections, prominent opposition figures have been charged with treason , allegations that they strongly reject. Among them is Tundu Lissu, the leader of opposition party Chadema, whom authorities blocked from running against Hassan last year. On Friday, Lissu was sentenced to four years in prison for “incitement” but acquitted of treason. “History will record how the court searched for a lesser charge simply to keep me in prison,” read a post published by Lissu’s team on his X account after the verdict. Fellow Chadema party member John Pambalu was charged with treason last month. Following Lissu’s verdict, Chadema on Sunday accused police of holding 10 of its leaders and members for “prolonged periods” without bringing them before a court, alleging torture and forced confessions. Rights groups, including Amnesty International, labeled Lissu’s trial “a deeply flawed process” and called on Tanzania’s government to “end the misuse of treason” and other laws to silence government critics. Tanzanian activists report a continuous wave of state-sponsored intimidation and abductions since last year’s election, allegations that the government denies. In July, authorities arrested more than 130 people over planned anti-government demonstrations—before they took place. The government had banned political rallies the previous month. “Young people are wandering around with no place where they feel they can speak out. When they do speak, they face intimidation. * Serbia’s Student Movement Faces Its Next Test
  Foreign Policy | 11 Nov 17:55 UTC
  The Student List made history, but can it govern?
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For nearly two years, student protesters demanded a fundamental transformation of Serbia’s political system, and the Oct. 25 elections marked an extraordinary step toward achieving it. Their movement defeated the formidable political machine of longtime leader Aleksandar Vucic in a stunning upset. The Student List secured approximately 53 percent of the vote and 140 parliamentary seats, with a record voter turnout of nearly 70 percent. It ended 14 years of uninterrupted rule by Vucic’s Serbian Progressive Party (SNS), which is left with 103 seats. A new government must be formed within 90 days of the official results. For nearly two years, student protesters demanded a fundamental transformation of Serbia’s political system, and the Oct. 25 elections marked an extraordinary step toward achieving it. Their movement defeated the formidable political machine of longtime leader Aleksandar Vucic in a stunning upset. The Student List secured approximately 53 percent of the vote and 140 parliamentary seats, with a record voter turnout of nearly 70 percent. It ended 14 years of uninterrupted rule by Vucic’s Serbian Progressive Party (SNS), which is left with 103 seats. A new government must be formed within 90 days of the official results. The victory is remarkable not least because the students disregarded almost every convention of electoral politics. They rejected traditional parties, eschewed recognizable leadership, and offered voters something closer to a moral crusade than a normal political program. The elections therefore functioned less as a choice between competing platforms and more as a referendum on Vucic’s years in power. Winning power and exercising it are different things. The qualities that made the student movement effective in opposition—from the students’ status as political outsiders to their ideological heterogeneity—could become liabilities in government. Now, the Student List must prove to an expectant public that it can move beyond deliberating what needs to change and govern. Serbia’s ongoing political upheaval traces back to November 2024, when a concrete canopy collapsed outside the newly renovated Novi Sad railway station, killing 16 people. Initial demonstrations demanded government accountability for the disaster and transparency over who was responsible for the construction and how public funds were spent. As protests spread across Serbia, the disaster became a symbol of broader anger over
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/11/serbia-parliament-elections-vucic-corruption-student-movement-sns-student-list/

* What a Hormuz Endgame Could Actually Look Like

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. With the prospect of renewed war in Iran seemingly off the table for now, and given the Trump administration’s redoubled commitment to economic pressure to overwhelm Tehran, the question again becomes: What will be the fate of the Strait of Hormuz? U.S. President Donald Trump, despite earlier threats to reignite hostilities in a last bid to cow Iran’s resistance, appears to have parked the military option. U.S. Secretary of State and National Security Advisor Marco Rubio also indicated that the double-whammy of Operation Economic Outcast (a naval blockade) and Iron Gate (a land blockade of Iranian trade) will bend the regime to the administration’s will. Not even angry phone calls with Israeli leadership appear able to summon the dogs of war again. That means that the overarching question—beyond the fate of Iran’s previously “obliterated” nuclear program, something that does not seem amenable to short-term diplomacy—is who will control the most strategic waterway on Earth. Before the war, which began in February, the Strait of Hormuz saw the transit of 20 percent of all seaborne oil exports in the world. It was never closed before, though there was a “tanker war” in the 1980s during the Iran-Iraq conflict that disrupted regional energy flows. But the strait was closed for much of this year, and it has only been prized open at great cost in recent months by the efforts of the U.S. Navy and the fortitude and deep pockets of seafarers and shipping companies. Oil flows out of Hormuz, and indeed the broader Middle East, are as high now, on average, as they were before the war began.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/11/strait-hormuz-endgame-iran-trump-control-oman-war-shipping-oil/

* Brazil Shows the Danger of Digital Chokepoints
  Foreign Policy | 11 Nov 15:22 UTC
  Lula’s campaign had to break the monopoly of Big Tech.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. When Brazilian President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva gave his victory speech in late October, he took one hand and held it aloft in tribute to “ our general ,” as his supporters cheered the “warrior of the Brazilian people.” The title was fittingly bestowed on André Janones, the 42-year-old congressman who vigorously mobilized almost 11 million voters as part of Lula’s “digital army,” marking the first time since Brazil’s return to democracy four decades ago that the runner-up surged ahead in the second round of a presidential election. It was a remarkable turnaround that will to be studied by election watchers for years to come. In one of the most consequential elections in Brazilian history, some three-quarters of the population were getting their news from social media, and Meta—the owner of the most prominent platforms—was supportive of Sen. Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of the now-imprisoned former president. Lula’s campaign was allegedly thwarted online on Meta’s platforms. New Delhi’s guarded optimism stems from a belief that a Democratic Congress might place some checks on the Trump administration’s propensity to make feckless decisions with adverse consequences for New Delhi. After all, the U.S.-India partnership enjoyed a bipartisan consensus for the last few decades, until Trump’s second term. Despite this sense of relief, Indian observers still harbor some qualms toward the United States. They are aware that any U.S. president wields substantial power in conducting foreign policy. * Memory Chips Are Completely Distorting South Korea’s Economy
  Foreign Policy | 11 Nov 09:11 UTC
  A booming tech industry is setting the country up for a potentially dramatic fall.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In South Korea, the term byeorak-geoji means “sudden beggar.” In 2026, it has gained widespread popularity again. But its meaning is figurative. Rather than denoting sudden bankruptcy or losing one’s life savings in the stock market, the term describes a new and widespread sense of relative deprivation: The realization that a colleague or acquaintance just earned years of your salary in a single month on paper. Such anxiety is driven by a booming domestic semiconductor industry that has a stranglehold on the global artificial intelligence industry’s supply bottleneck : memory chips. At the market’s peak this year, Samsung Electronics saw its market capitalization surge to a record high of $1.3 trillion, while SK Hynix has since reported a staggering 328 percent year-on-year increase in revenue in its latest third-quarter results. The boom had led many Koreans to believe that borrowing to leverage into these companies offered the quickest and perhaps the only route to profits large enough to pay off their mortgages and secure homes. In South Korea, the term byeorak-geoji means “sudden beggar.” In 2026, it has gained widespread popularity again. But its meaning is figurative. Rather than denoting sudden bankruptcy or losing one’s life savings in the stock market, the term describes a new and widespread sense of relative deprivation: The realization that a colleague or acquaintance just earned years of your salary in a single month on paper. Such anxiety is driven by a booming domestic semiconductor industry that has a stranglehold on the global artificial intelligence industry’s supply bottleneck : memory chips. At the market’s peak this year, Samsung Electronics saw its market capitalization surge to a record high of $1.3 trillion, while SK Hynix has since reported a staggering 328 percent year-on-year increase in revenue in its latest third-quarter results. The boom had led many Koreans to believe that borrowing to leverage into these companies offered the quickest and perhaps the only route to profits large enough to pay off their mortgages and secure homes. If you were an engineer at one of these companies, you could cash out stock compensation boosted by stock rallies fueled by people’s borrowing, collect enormous bonuses, and secure a mortgage rate of 1.5 percent through company housing-loan programs, alongside support for your children’s tuition and retirement savings. Meanwhile, ordinary Koreans carrying deb
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/11/south-korea-semiconductor-memory-chips-artificial-intelligence-inflation-affordability/

* Analysis: Climate-finance pledges fall 70% as countries meet at COP31
  Carbon Brief | 11 Nov 07:00 UTC
  The amount of public funding pledged to help developing countries tackle climate change has dropped by more than 70% in… The post Analysis: Climate-finance pledges fall 70% as countries meet at COP31 appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The amount of public funding pledged to help developing countries tackle climate change has dropped by more than 70% in 2026, according to Carbon Brief analysis. Developed countries are required under UN climate treaties to provide “ climate finance ” and have traditionally announced multi-year pledges to feed into their global commitments. Most of these pledges expired in 2025 and, since then, the US has withdrawn from international climate efforts and many European aid budgets have been cut. A civil-society tracker shows that only five nations have announced new multi-year pledges, including just one major donor – the UK. Germany, France and Japan are among the key contributors that have so far failed to set out plans for new climate finance, while none is expected from the US. As a result, the total amount of public climate finance pledged over the next few years amounts to just $11.7bn a year in spending, down from recent levels of around $40bn. Experts tell Carbon Brief that global conflict, fiscal constraints and the rise of the far right are all making it harder for developed-country governments to pledge new funds. As the COP31 summit in Turkey gets underway, developing countries are stressing their urgent need for new finance, after nations agreed to a new global target just two years ago. Under the Paris Agreement , developed countries are obliged to provide climate finance to help developing countries cut their emissions and prepare for climate disasters. This financial assistance can be channelled through multilateral development banks , UN climate funds and investments “mobilised” from the private sector. But a large chunk is also expected to come directly from the public coffers of developed countries – primarily their foreign-aid budgets. This public spending has made up roughly a third of climate finance flowing to developing countries in recent years. The EU, the US and others agreed just two years ago that they would “take the lead” on scaling up annual climate finance from $100bn in the early 2020s to $300bn by 2035. Donor countries have traditionally supported such goals by announcing multi-year targets that indicate how much they plan to spend on climate finance. Nearly all of these pledges expired in 2025 – and hardly any major donors have announced new ones to replace them, covering the period out to 2030. This lack of new commitments is captured in a tracker of old and new financial commitments by climate NGOs NRDC and E3G . Carbon 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-climate-finance-pledges-fall-70-as-countries-meet-at-cop31

--- Editorial (44) ---

* Rays of Wonder
  Longreads | 11 Nov 18:51 UTC
  "Grappling with the Paradox of 'Do No Harm.'"
  FULL TEXT: Hanna Saltzman sets a captivating history of radiology alongside that of her family, generations of medical professionals who championed the benevolent powers of x-rays and radiation and also suffered their damages. Her essay for the new issue of Virginia Quarterly Review wrestles with the impossibility of perfect vision and the inadvertent harms we visit on those who we most want to protect. Within months of Röntgen’s discovery, the unintended consequences of the X-ray began to emerge. In Tennessee, a boy came to the hospital after an accidental gunshot wound to his head. To test the equipment before trying it on his patient, his physician first conducted a prolonged X-ray of his own head. Three weeks later, the physician lost his hair. That same year, a New York doctor conducting X-ray experiments on himself noticed that his fingernails had stopped growing, his knuckles burned, and even the skin of his hand began to peel away. Blisters bloomed on other people’s bodies—on their hands and arms and chests. Thomas Edison started feeling cramps in his stomach; his vision began to blur. His glassblower, Clarence Dally, tested the quality of the fluoroscope tubes through daily X-rays of his own body. His hair started falling out, and his eyelashes and eyebrows burned away. His hands became red, swollen, and ulcerated. Knobs of deformed tissue bulged from his fingers. A biopsy of Dally’s pinky revealed cancer. . . . When Edison saw Dally’s tumors, he swore to never again expose himself or anyone else to radiation. In the year before Dally’s death, as the doomed glassblower lost himself to the disease, the New York World ran a story on the gruesome costs of this celebrated breakthrough. “Don’t talk to me about X-rays,” the prolific inventor told the reporter. “I am afraid of them.” “Meeting your kids in the Forgotten Realms.” “On cartoons, colors, ferris wheels, Father’s Day, Prince, coming out, the internet, and me.” “The first time I try to visit the devil, I bring my son.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/11/x-rays-radiology-cancer-history-rontgen/

* After the Midterms, America Is Getting Politicians Who Live Like Me
  New Lines | 11 Nov 14:16 UTC
  The recent successes of Muslim progressive candidates like Abdul El-Sayed, Adam Hamawy and Zohran Mamdani are symptoms of the slow death of identity politics in America. The post After the Midterms, America Is Getting Politicians Who Live Like Me appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: I recently told my parents that I regret the hundreds of pepperoni pizza slices I passed on throughout the ’90s. All the birthday parties, last day of school parties, middle school play cast parties — “I grieve the loss of the flavors I never had!” I exclaimed. Their sympathies typically fall short of my emotional needs. My parents, Egyptian immigrants, raised my sister and me in the Muslim faith. We memorized passages of the Quran, visited the mosque and, yes, abstained from pork due to the association between its consumption and an eternity in hell. This was particularly painful to me because Michelangelo, my favorite “Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles” character, loved pepperoni pizza. Now, as a nearly 40-year-old man, I’m not sure I’d call myself a “believer” in Islam — hence the regret — but I still feel like a Muslim. Because, even if I turned out to be a pepperoni-eating heathen, I still huddled with my wife and led us in the recitation of my mental catalog of Quran passages before she was rolled into the operating room for her cesarean section. I still pray two rakat (prostrations) before bed if I’m feeling a little lost, or put on audio from the holy book when I can’t sleep. That’s why I bristle when non-Muslim friends push back on my identification as a Muslim with smug reminders of lifestyle choices like drinking alcohol, or eating bacon, egg and cheese sandwiches. These experiences came to mind when I watched Tommy Teepell, a former staffer for far-right Sen. Josh Hawley, ask the Democrats’ then-nominee for U.S. Senate, Abdul El-Sayed, to disavow the Muslim Brotherhood. These kinds of comments stem from the same American propensity to replace the character, individuality and complexity of the marginalized with their identity. But El-Sayed is riding last week’s blue wave to Congress as the soon-to-be first Muslim senator in history, while Teepell’s plan backfired as a blatantly racist stunt that emphasized labels over values. If anything, Teepell’s far-right positions on women’s rights, the LGBTQ+ community, and the fusion of church and state qualify him as an ideal recruit for the Muslim Brotherhood. Indeed, El-Sayed was onto something when he retorted, “Have you disavowed them?” The recent successes of Muslim progressive candidates like El-Sayed, Adam Hamawy in New Jersey and New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani are symptoms of the slow death of identity politics in America. Muslims candidates and politicians on the left do not primarily associate th
  https://newlinesmag.com/running-notes/after-the-midterms-america-is-getting-politicians-who-live-like-me/

Together, the Jewish-Zionist and non-Zionist Arab parties have a comfortable 67-seat majority, more than enough to form a government. A government with the Arab parties, which together won 19 seats, or even a minority government kept alive by their votes from outside the coalition, has been taboo in Israeli politics for decades. The right’s campaign to delegitimize Arab participation in government has thus achieved what Netanyahu could not achieve at the ballot box, which is to help keep Netanyahu in power.   FULL TEXT: Twenty-two years since their emergence as a rebel group in Yemen, the Houthis have become arguably the most powerful nonstate actor in the world. How and why did they manage such a feat? Yet Iranian support, and the Yemeni army arsenal they seized, are not the only factors behind the Houthis’ strength. Their enemies’ strategic mistakes, miscalculations, and absence of coherent strategies and policies — along with incompetence, inefficiency and divisions among local rivals — also boosted and emboldened them. Hussein al-Houthi was a member of Parliament between 1993 and 1997. Saleh considered him and his supporters a challenge to his authority in Saada. Mediation efforts aimed at convincing them to hand themselves over to the police were unsuccessful, and clashes erupted on June 18, 2004, as government forces attempted to arrest the Houthi leader. Mediation continued even after the clashes, but al-Houthi refused to surrender. On Sept. 10, the government announced the killing of the rebel leader and the end of the insurgency. * Equality talk
  Aeon | 09 Nov 11:00 UTC
  In the summer of 1646, the idea of equality gained new and profound depths. We are still catching up with the consequences - by Teresa M Bejan Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Striking dockworkers, England, 1954. Photo by Marc Riboud/Magnum Photos It’s no accident that equality comes first among the ‘self-evident’ truths articulated in the United States Declaration of Independence. The claim that ‘all men are created equal’ serves there not simply as an abstract premise from which revolutionary consequences ought to follow, but as a concrete practical foundation on which the American colonists could claim to stand as equals with the greatest king and hold him to account. Still impressed by his own handiwork 50 years later, Thomas Jefferson even prophesied that the ‘palpable truth’ of equality enshrined in the Declaration would one day unite all of humankind. Yet today, it’s evident that the word ‘equality’ means very different things to different people. What once seemed capable of providing common ground is now just another source of controversy. What explains this shift? I suspect that the deceptively simple, conceptual language of ‘equality’ has itself played a substantial role. A better understanding of ‘equality talk’ – as the application of this language to human beings and their relations – might therefore shed some light on our predicament. L et’s begin by examining a group of people that generally prides itself on thinking (and speaking) more clearly than most: professional philosophers. What are they doing when they talk about equality? Often, they’re saying something about human beings: about what we’re fundamentally like, and how we should be treated morally, politically and socially. When an academic philosopher says that ‘all people are equal’, she’s generally saying that, despite differences of race and gender, economic class or individual merit, every person is somehow the same in some fundamental respect(s). While some philosophers will stress the human capacity for reason and moral agency, others emphasise our mutual vulnerability to suffering and violent death. Still, all generally agree that this sameness – whatever its underlying basis – ought to matter for our social and political arrangements, such that any persistent inequalities between human beings must require some justification or redress. But here, a non-philosopher might wonder why the professionals insist on using the technical language of ‘equality’ as opposed to something more straightforward like ‘sameness’?   https://aeon.co/essays/historical-ideas-of-equality-help-us-imagine-an-equal-world

* The Poppy’s Many Meanings
  New Lines | 06 Nov 11:42 UTC
  The flower is used to commemorate sacrifice in war, but the humble weed has far more negative connotations in some countries. The post The Poppy’s Many Meanings appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: A planted seed is generally left well alone to sprout and grow in the darkness, perhaps with the help of water or fertilizer. But there is a notable exception: Poppies require light to germinate, meaning the earth must be disturbed before they grow. This feature of the flower explains much about its ubiquity and patterns across landscapes. It appears in particular density in places where earth has been turned over, such as farmland or cemeteries — or where trenches have been dug to protect troops in war. Nov. 11 is Remembrance Day across the Commonwealth, with poppies seen on lapels throughout the month, and memorial events held on the Sunday closest to the day itself — this year, this coming Sunday. These spots of color on coats symbolize remembrance of, and gratitude to, those who lost their lives fighting in war, drawing on images from World War I of the poppies that sprouted, undeterred by violence, all along the front lines of Europe. These front lines shifted regularly, often by tiny amounts, but each time requiring more trenches to be dug and more soil to be disrupted — so that more poppies grew. Poppies on battlefields had been noted during the Napoleonic Wars of the early 19th century, but the image was cemented in the minds of generations of schoolchildren by a poem scribbled in the back of an ambulance during World War I, by Canadian physician John McCrae. It begins: “In Flanders fields, the poppies blow / Between the crosses, row on row / That mark our place.” Its patriotism, signaling the rightness of dying for one’s country, is suitable for the military parades of Remembrance Sunday, and the simplicity and the color of the poppy make it an ideal emblem, adopted in 1921 by the newly formed Royal British Legion to fundraise to help those who served in the war. Neither the poem nor the poppy are uncontroversial, however. “Take up our quarrel with the foe!” the last verse begins, signaling a commitment to conflict many find abhorrent; these people shun the poppy or instead choose to wear a white version, symbolizing peace rather than war. But there are far wider problems, worldwide, with what poppies represent. From the opium wars in China to the poppy fields of India and Afghanistan to the opioid crisis in America, the poppy has been a source of addiction, conflict and misery — and, for a few, wealth. And these dichotomies, between wealth and grinding poverty, memories of noble sacrifice or of oppression and addiction, are not the only meanings 
  https://newlinesmag.com/essays/the-poppys-many-meanings/

* Celestial nuisance
  Aeon | 06 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The cosmos has become a lab for the Big Questions in physics. Along the way, we’ve lost sight of its glorious messiness - by Dylan Jow Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Journey to a satellite galaxy roughly 200,000 light-years from Earth, featuring the young star cluster NGC 602. With data from Webb’s NIRCam ( Near-InfraRed Camera ) and MIRI ( Mid-InfraRed Instrument ). Courtesy HUBBLE/WEBB/ESA In the story of cosmic history, one of the most important supporting characters is dust. As clouds of interstellar gas cooled and condensed in the early Universe, dust grains absorbed and re-emitted radiation, eventually forming stars. Later, dust provided the raw material from which planets could form and grow. Dust grains may even have been the surfaces on which the first complex organic molecules formed. Yet, if you ask most cosmologists, they’ll tell you that dust is a sheer nuisance. In astrophysics, ‘nuisance’ is a quasi-technical term. Astrophysicists call any phenomenon that gets in the way of the thing they actually want to study a ‘nuisance’. Dust grains emit polarised light that can mimic subtle signals from the afterglow of the Big Bang (the cosmic microwave background) that cosmologists have spent decades and billions of dollars trying to detect. A cosmologist studies dust not to uncover its role in shaping the cosmos, but to subtract it – the term ‘sweeping under the rug’ couldn’t be more apt. This dichotomy between ‘signal’ and ‘nuisance’ is found across many sub-fields in astronomy. Astronomers who study a phenomenon called ‘weak lensing’ use the subtle distortions of light caused by gravity to map invisible dark matter. For them, the ordinary matter that makes up everything we can see is a nuisance. Pulsar timing astronomers use rapidly spinning neutron stars called pulsars as extraordinarily precise clocks to detect the passage of gravitational waves through the galaxy. For them, the nuisance is ionised interstellar gas, which scatters and disperses the light from pulsars. Yet one astronomer’s nuisance is another’s treasure. Just as a laser beam reveals the intricate swirls of smoke in a dark room, pulsars illuminate the fine structure of the interstellar medium, the unlit space between galaxies. If you want to study the interstellar medium and its role in shaping our galaxy’s evolution, the light-scattering gas is an invaluable resource. The ESO-operated Very Large Telescope based at the Cerro Paranal site in the Atacama Desert of northern Chile. Photo by Miguel Claro/ESO Whether a phenomenon is a nuisance or something worth studying in its own right clearly depends on the investigator and their interests. But in
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-astrophysics-needs-to-abandon-the-big-questions

The fix O’Gorman endorses may sound drastic, but his precautionary principle is shared by invasion biology, the subdiscipline devoted to the study of species transplanted from their native ranges. There is a long history of organisms moved by humans, whether deliberately or inadvertently, wreaking total havoc upon their new homes. The brown tree snake, native to Indonesia, northern Australia, and Papua New Guinea, was accidentally introduced to Guam by the US military following World War II and promptly ate its way through ten of the island’s twelve endemic forest bird species. Similarly, Nile perch released into Lake Victoria by British colonial forces gulped down roughly two hundred species of homegrown cichlids, fish found nowhere else on Earth. Cane toads in Australia, Miconia trees on Hawaii and Tahiti, rats just about everywhere—the list goes on. And if biodiversity loss, however regrettable, seems like an abstraction in the scheme of things, know that human culture frequently suffers direct impoverishments because of invasives. Chestnut blight, discovered in the United States in 1904 after chestnut trees were imported from Japan, effectively deleted the American chestnut tree from the continent; its products were central to the diet and lifestyles of various indigenous peoples and communities across Appalachia. Phylloxera, an aphid relative, was imported to Europe on American grapevines during the second half of the nineteenth century. The subsequent infestation resulted in the destruction, by some estimates, of 90 percent of all European vineyards. “Godwits make the longest non-stop migration in the bird world. That might be the least of their marvels. On Oct. 27, the Iran-backed group stated that they consider Cairo’s decision to aid Riyadh a “declaration of war.” The statement came a day after Egypt’s Parliament approved Sisi’s request to provide military support to Saudi Arabia if called upon to do so. Ahmed Aboudouh, an associate fellow of the Middle East and North Africa Program at Chatham House, believes that Egypt is preparing to proactively manage the risks. He told New Lines that the operations were likely to be defensive, but that this could mean not only defending from within Saudi territory, but also defending Egypt by attempting to limit the Houthis’ ability to attack Egyptian targets. “My sense is that Egypt’s role will be confined to air and naval defense,” Aboudouh added. He explained that Saudi Arabia’s vast size and critical assets make it difficult to intercept the barrage of Houthi airstrikes — a challenge that Egypt could help with. On Oct. 10, the Houthis hit Riyadh International Airport , killing 13 people and injuring more than 300. It was the deadliest attack on the kingdom in more than a decade. In addition, Aboudouh expects Egyptian naval vessels to enter the Bab al-Mandab Strait to protect the strategic route from Houthi attacks. * Burying the Evidence
  Longreads | 05 Nov 10:00 UTC
  The future doesn’t care about your time capsule, but don’t let that stop you.
  FULL TEXT: Longreads has published hundreds of original stories—personal essays, reported features, reading lists, and more—and more than 14,000 editor’s picks. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. Ashley Stimpson | Longreads | November 5, 2026 | 18 minutes (4,985 words) In the months leading up to the nation’s bicentennial, the rickety wooden wheels of covered wagons began to roll in every corner of the United States—from the evergreen forests of Washington and windswept prairies of North Dakota to the granite peaks of Maine and palm tree-lined suburbs of Central Florida. Over the next year, the Bicentennial Wagon Train (BWT) would commemorate the founding of the country by performing the reverse of Westward Expansion, as five caravans—eventually made up of as many as 300 horse-drawn wagons—snaked their way toward Valley Forge, where they would arrive on July 4, 1976, just in time to celebrate America’s 200th birthday. Along the way, the wagon trains would stop in hundreds of cities and towns, where residents could watch teams of wagoneers construct historically accurate encampments and perform a musical revue. If so moved, citizens could also sign a so-called rededication scroll, to recommit themselves to building a more perfect union. “To commemorate this nation’s Bicentennial, we hereby dedicate ourselves anew to the precepts of our Founding Fathers,” each signer promised, inking their name on a sheath of scratchy parchment paper. Large community dinners and solemn ceremonies were planned around these vows to democracy; in some cities, whole elementary schools and entire churches lined up to sign. To accommodate those not on the wagons’ path, scroll-signing centers were set up in Holiday Inns from coast to coast. The scrolls were then loaded onto the Mayflower moving van that accompanied each train and carried toward Pennsylvania, where organizers planned to have them microfilmed and placed in an aluminum time capsule interred in the Pennsylvania dirt for the next 100 years. In 2076, organizers of the Bicentennial Wagon Train imagined, future Americans would unearth these scrolls and feel a deep sense of appreciation and admiration for the Americans of the past. Altogether, 22 million people (a tenth of the population at the time) signed approximately 917,000 scrolls, a stack of paper as high as a 20-story building . By all accounts these scrolls really did end up in Pennsylvania, where President Gerald Ford signed one, right before 
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/05/time-capsules-ashley-stimpson/
